The petitioners are local candidates of State of
Andhra Pradesh and are aspirants seeking admission to
MBBS/BDS Courses in the State of Telangana uflder the
non-local category. In these petitions, the petitioners have
impugned the validity of Rules (3XIDG), @) (h) and Rule
(3)(III)(a) which have been substituted vide G.O.Ms.No.72,
dated O3.O7.2O23, in Telangana Medical & Dental Colleges
Admission (Admission into MBBS & BDS Courses) Rules,
2O17 (hereinafter referred to as 'the 2017 RulesJ. In order

4
to appreciate petitioners' grievance, reference to few facts is
necessary which are stated infra.
{iI FACTUAL MATRIX:
2. The factual matrix lies in a narrow compass. The
Parliament enacted National Medical Commission Act,
2019. Under the Act, National Medical Council was
constituted with effect from 25.09.1990. The National
Testing Agency (NTA) issued a norilication on O6.O3.2U23.
In pursuance of the aforesaid notification, the petitioners
as well as other candidates submitted their applications
between 6tr, March, 2023 arrd 6rh April, 2023 for appearing
in National Eligibitity-cum-Entrance Test undergraduate
examination (hereinafter referred to as .NEET UGJ. NEET
UG examination was held on OT.OS.2O23. The results of
the said examination were declared on 13.06.2023.
Thereafter, the Rules were amended on O3.O2.2O23 by
which IOO% reservation has been provided in respect of
85% of competent authority quota seats in favour of local
candidates in educational institutions established after
02.06.2014 i.e., the date_of formarion ot State of Telangana.

5
Thereafter, the University issued notification on
06.07.2023 inviting online applica.tions for admission to
MBBS/BDS courses in the State of Telangana.
3. In the aforesaid factual background, the challenge
has been made to validity of Rules (3XIU (d), (e), (h) and
Rule (3)(lll)(a) of the 2Ol7 Rules which have been
substituted, vide G.O. Ms. N o.7 2, dated O 3.O7 .2023.

petitioners in this batch of writ petitions had filed a writ
petition under Article 32 of the Constitution of India which
was registered as W.P (Civil) No.916 of 2023. The aforesaid
writ petition was dismissed by Honble Supreme Court
dated 31.08.2023. The said order reads as under:
"1. Heard learned counsel for ttre petitioners and
the learned counscl for the intervenors/impleadors.
2. l*arr.cd counsels have not satisfied us as to
the reason for frling the petition under Article 32 of
the Constitution o[ [nd.ia, particularly when the writ
invotving similar questions are pending before the
High Court.

6
3. [rarned counsels state that t]rere is urgency
in the matter. If that be so, we see no reason why the
High Court would not take up the matter
expeditiously to pass interim orders if tl-e Court
consiclers it appropriate and in accorda-nce with [aw.
4. The writ petition and the apptications for
intervention/impteadment and direction are.
accordingly, dismissed-5. Pending application (s), if any, shall stand
disposed of."

22.
The State Legislature in exercise o[ powers conferred
by Entry 25 of the Concurrent List to the Seventh Schedule

t9
of the Con
Telangana
stitution of India has enacted arl. Act, namely
Educational lnstitutions (Regulation of Admission and
Section 3 of
Prohibition of Capitation Fee) Act, 1983.
the 1983 Act deals with regulation of admission into educational institutions. Section 3 enables
the Government to frame Rules. Section IS of the 19g3 Act
deals with powers of the State Government to rnake rules for carrying out the purposes of the Act. In exercise of
powers under Section 3 read r.t ith Section l5(l) of the 19g3
Act, the 2Ol7 Rules have been framed.
24. From perusat of paragraph 3 of G.O.Ms.No.ll4,
dated 05.O7.2O12 reads as under:
NOTIFtCATION
3. In exercise o[ the powers conferred by

(Regulation of Admission and prohibition of

into MBBS & BDS Courses) Rules' 2O17'
25. Thus, it is evident that the 2017 Rules have been
framed under the 1983 Act' Section 15 of Telangana
General Clauses Act, 13O8 Fasli provides that power to
make rules includes the power to add' vary or rescind'
Therefore, the State Government has power to amend the
2O t7 Rules also ' The validity o[ neither Section 3 nor
Section 15 of the 1983 Act has been assailed by the
petitioners in these Petitions'
26. The relevant extract of Article 35 of the Constitution
of India reads as under
35. Legistation to grve effect to the
provisions of this Part- Notwithstanding anything in
this Constitution,-
(a)l Parliament shall have, and the legislature of a
State sha-ll not have, power to make laws-
(i) *ith respect to arly of the matters which
under clause (3) of article 16, clause (3) of articlc 32.

article 33 and article 34 may be provided for. by law
made by Parliament; and
(ii) for prescribing punishment for those acts
which are declared to be offences under this Part,
And Parliament shall, as soon as may be after
the commencement o[ this Constitution, makes laws
for prescribing punishment for the acts referred to in
sub-clause (ii)
27. Article 16 of the Constitution of [ndia dea]s with
equality of opportunity in the matters of public
employment. Article 16(3) reads as under:
16r Equality of opportunity in matters of
public emPlo5rment.- (1) xxx
(2) xxx
(3) Nothing in this article shall prevent
Parliament from making any law prescribing, in
regard to a class or classes of employment or
appointment to an officc under the Government of, or
any local or other authority within, a State or Union
territory, any requirement as to residence within that
State or Union Territory prior to such emplo5rment or
appointment-2a. Thus, perusa-t of Article 35(a) in conjunction with
Article 16 makes it clear that Parliament is competent to
make any law with regard to class or classes of
employment or for appointment to an office under the

22
Government of, any local or other authority within a State
or Union Territory, any requirernent as to residence within
that State or Union Territory prior to such employment or
appointment. Neither the l9g3 Act nor ttre 2Ol7 Rules or
arnendment thereof deal with employment or appointment
to an office . Therefore, the contention that under Article
16(3) of the Constitution of Ind.ia parliament a-lone has
power to make the impugned Rules and State tegislature
has no competence to amend the 2OLZ Rule s is
misconceived. Therefore, the issue No.(i) is answered in the
affirmative by stating that the State [rgislature is
competent to amend the 2Ol7 Rules.

46. Legitimate or reasonable expectation may arise from
an express promise given on behalf of a public authority- It
may also arise from existence of a regular practice which a
claimant can reasonably expect to continue (See R v.
Secretary of Transport Exporte Greater London
Councilr3). In Bannari Amman Sugars Ltd. v.
Commercial Tax Oflicerr4, the Hon,ble Supreme Court
u,hile dealing with the doctline of legitimate expectation in
paragraph 20 held as under:
20. ln Shrijee Scles Corpn. v. Ilnion of
Indiq l(19971 3 SCC 3981 it was observcd thar once
public interest is accepted as the superior cquity which
can override individual equity the principle would bc
applicable even in cases where a period has been
indicated for operation of the promise. I[ there is a
supen,ening public equity, the Government would be
allowed to change its stand and has the power to
wrthdraw from represcntation made by it which induced
pcrsons to take certain steps which may havc gonc
adverse to the interest of such persons on account of
such withdrawal. Moreover, the Governmcnt is
'3 11985) 3 A .ER 3OO
L. (2OOs) I SCC 625

37
competent to rescind from the promise even if there is
no manifest public interest involved, provided no one is
put in any adverse situation which cannot be recCiFred.
Similar view was expressed in Pawa.n Atlogs and
Co'stilng e) Ltd. v. U.P. SEB[(1997|. 7 SCC 251 : AIR
L997 SC 39101 and in SIlOv. Shree Durga Oil
llfttls [(1998) I SCC 5721 and it was further held that
the Govemment could change its industrial policy if the
situation so wa-rranted and merely because the
resolution was announced for a particular period, it did
not mean that the Government could not amend and
change the policy under any circumstances- If the party
claiming application of the doctrine acted on the basis of
a notifrcation it should havc known that such
notifrcation was liable to be amendcd or rescinded at
any point of time, if the Government felt that it was
necessary to do so in public intercsl .
47. A Constitution Bench of the Hon'ble Supreme Court
in Sivanandan C.T v. High Court of Keralars dealt with
the doctrine of legitimate expectation. In paragraphs 18,
29, 4L and 60 of decision, it is held as under:
18. The basis of the doctrine o[ legitimate expectation
in public law is founded on the principles of fairness
and non-arbitrariness in government dealings with
individuals. It recognizes that a public authority's
prornise or past conduct wilt give rise to a legitimate
expectation. The doctrine is premised on the notion that
ts 2023 SCC Online SC 994

l8
public authorities, while performing their public duties,
ought to honor their promises or past practices. The
legitimacy of al expectation can be inferred if it is rooted
in law, custom, or established procedure. (Salemi v.
Macke llar l|l9 7 4 I{CA 261.
29. A claim based on the doctrine of procedural
legitimate cxpectation a_rises where a claimant expects
the public auttrority to follow a particular procedure
bcforc taking a decision. This is in contradistinction to
the doctrine of substantive legitimate expectation wherc
a claimant expects conferra_l of a substantive beneht
based on the exrsting promise or practice of the public
authority- The doctrine of substantive legitimate
expectation has now been accepted as an integral part of
both ttre common law as well as Indian jutrisprudence.
4L. Thc doctrine of legitimate e*pectation docs not
impr:de or hinder the power of the public authoritics to
lay down a policy or withdrdw it. The public authoriry
lras the discretion to exercise the full range of choiccs
avarlable within its executive power. The public
authority often has to take into consideration diverse
factors, concerns, and interests before arriving at a
particular policy decision. The courts are generally
cautious in interfering with a bona fide decision of
public authorities which denies a legitimate expectation
proi,ided such a decision is taken in the larger pubtic
intercst. Thus, public interest serves as a limitation on
thc application of the doctrine of legitimate expectation.
Courts have to determine whether the public interest is
compclling and sufi-rcient to outweigh the legitimatc
cxpcctation of thc claimJnt. While performing ir

i9
balancing exercise, courts have to often grapple.with the
issues o[ burden ald standard of proof required to
dislodge the claim of legitimate expectation.
6O. The following are our conclusions in view of the
above discussions:
(r) The principles of good administration require tJlat
the decisions of public authorities must
withstand the test of consistency, transparency,
and predictability to avoid being termed as
arbitrary and violative of Article 14;
(ii) An individual who claims a benefit or entitlement
based on the doctrine of substantive legitimale
expectation has to establish the following : (i) the
legitimacy of the expectation; and that (ii) the
denial of the legitimate expectation led to a
violation of Article 14;
(iii) A public authority must objectively demonstrate
by placing relevant material before the court that
its decision was in the public interest to frustrate
a clairn of legitimate expectation;
(iv) The decision of the High Court of Kerda to apply
a minirnum cut-off to the viva voce exa.nination is
contrary to Rule 2(c)(iii) of the 1961 Rules.
{v) Thc High Court's decision to apply the minknum
cut-off marks for the viva voce frustrates the
substantive legrtimate expectation of the
petitioners. The decision is arbitrar5r and violative
of Arl icle I4.

40
(vi) In terms of relief, we hold that it w_ould be
contrary to the public interest to direct the
induction of the petitioners into the Higher
Judicial Service after the lapse of more than six
years. Candidates who have been selected nearly
six years ago cannot be unseated. They were all
qualified and have becn serving the districr
judiciary of the state. Unseating them at this
stage would be contrary to public intercst. To
induct the petitioners would be to bring in new
candidates in preference to those who are holding
ludicial office for a length of time. To deprive the
state and its citizrns of the benefit of these
experienced judicial officers at a senior position
would not be in public interest.

existence after 02.06.2O14. By amending the 2017 Rules

49. In Madras City Wine Merchants' Association v
State of Tamil Nadu16, the Honble Supreme Court in
paragraph 52 hetd that doctrine of legitimate expectation
applies only in the held of administrative law ald it is not
permissible to invoke the doctrine as against the
Iegislation. Relevant extract of paragraph 52 reads as
under:
52. It was by a rule (subordinate legislation) in
exercise of thc powers conferred kry Sections 17 -C, L7-
t6 (t9941 5 SCC 5O9

42
D, 2I and 54 of the Tamil Nadu prohibition Act, 1937
licences under Bar Rules came to be granted . Those
Rulcs have been repealed by exercise of the same
powers under Sections 17 -C, 17 -D, 2 I and 54 of thc
Prohibition Act. Therefore, this is a case of legislation.
The doctrine of legitimate expectation arises only. in
the field of administrative decisions. It the plea of
legitimate expectation relates to procedural fairness
there is no possibility whatever of invoking the
doctrine as against the legislation...
50. Therefore, in view of the aforesaid enunciation of law
by Hont-rle Supreme Court, it is evident that the doctrine of
legitimate expectation cannot be invoked against
legidation. Therefore, the issue No.{iv} iS dnswered by
stating that the petitioners did not have any legitimate
expectation under Section 95 of the Reorganisation Act and
therefore, the question of its violation by amendment of the
2O I7 Rules does not arise.

52. [n Pradeep Jain (supra), a three-Judge Bench of
Hon'ble Supreme Court dealt with the question whether
admission to a medical collegE or any other institution of
higher learning situated in the State can be confined to
those who have their domicile within the State or who are
resident within the State for a specihed number of years or
can any reservation in admissions be made for them so as
to give them precedence over those who do not possess
domicile or residential qualification within the State
irrespective of merit. The
i
tron'ble Supreme Court in

45
paragraph 2O, which is extracted below for the facility of
reference, held as under:
20. The only question which remains to be
considered is as to what should be the extent of
reservation based on residence requirement and
institutional preference. There can be no doubt that
such reservation cannot completely exclude admission
of students from other universities and States on the
basis of merit judged in open competition. Krishna
Iyer, J., rightly remarked in Jagdish Saran
case [(1980) 2 SCC 768 : AIR 198O SC 82O : (1980) 2
SCR 83fl at pages 845 and 846 of the Report: (SCC p.
778, para 221
"... reservation must be kept in check by
the demands of competence. You cannot
extend the shelter o[ reservation where
minimum qua-lifications are absent,
Sirnilarly, all the best talent cannot be
completely excluded by wholesale
reservation. So, a certain percentage, which
may be available, must be kept open for
meritorious performance regardless of
university, State and the like. Complete
exclusion of the rest of the country [or the
sake of a province, wholesale banishment of
proven ability to open up, hopefully,
some dalit talent, total sacrifrce o[
excellence at the altar of equalisation
-
when the Constitution mandates for every
one equality before and equal protection o[
the law
-
may be fatal folly, se[f-defeating

46
educational technolos/ and antinationa_[ if
made a routine rule of State policy. A fai r
preference, a reasonable reservation, a just
adjustment o[ the prior needs and real
potential of the weak with the partial
recognition of the presence of competitive
merit
-
such is the dynamics of social
justice which animates the three egalitarian
articles of the ConsLitution."
We agree whotly with these observations made by the
learned Judge and we unreservedly condemn
uholesale reseraation made by some of the State
Governments on the basis of "domicile" or residence
requirement within the State or on the basis of
institutional prcference, for students who have passed
the qualifying examination held by the university or
thc Statc excluding all students not satistying this
requirement, regardless of rmerit- We declare such
wholesale reservation to be unconstitutiona] and void
as being in violation of Article l4 of the Constitution.
53. The aforesaid decision in pradeep Jain (supra) was
considered by another three-Judge Bench in Reita
Nirankari (supra). The order passed in Reita Nirankari
(supra) supra reads as under:
I. Some o[ thc students seeking admission to thc
MBBS coursc in this academic year have made an
application to this Court that the Judgment delivercd
on June 22, 1984 [ Pradccp Jain v Union of India.

47
(1984) 3 SCC 6541 in the medical admission cases
may be given effect to only from the next academic
year, because admissions have already been made in

Universities in the country prior to the delivery of the
Judgment on June 22, 1984 and moreover, some time
would be required for the purpose o[ achieving
uniformity in the procedure relating to admissions in
the various Universities. We accordingly issued notice
on the application to the learned advocates who had
appeared on behalf of the various parties at the
hearing of the main writ petitions as also to the
Attorney General ald after hearing them, we have
come to the conclusion and this is accepted by all
parties that in view of the fact that all formalitics for
admission, including the holding of entrance
examination, have been completed in some of the
States prior to the Judgment dated J,une 22, L984
and also since some time would be required for
making the necessaqr preparations for implementing
the judgment, it is not practicable to give effect to thc
judgment from the present academic year and in fact
compelling some States to give effect to the Judgrnent
from the present academ.ic year when others have not,
would result in producing inequality and if all the
States were to be required to implement the judgment
immediately, admissions already rnade would have to
be cancetled and fre sh entrance examinations would
have to be held and this would require at least 2 or
2 y2 months delaying the commencement of t}Ie
academic term apart from causing immense hardship
to the students. We therefore dircct that thc judgment

48
shall be implemented with effect from the next
academic year 1985-86. Whatever admissions,
provisional or otherwise, have been made for the
academic year 1984-85, shall not be disturbed on the
basis of the judgment. We may make it clear that the
judgment will not apply to the States of Andhra
Pradesh and Jammu and Kashmir because at the
(lme ol hearing o[ the main writ petitions, it was
pointed out to us by the learned advocates appearing
on bchalf of those States that there were special
constitutional provisions in regard to ttrem which
woulcl need independent consideration by this Court.
2. This orde r will form part of the main judgment
dclivcred on June 22, 19A4.

19. Aftcr the said judgment was delivered, the

49
in Reito Nirankoi [Reita Nirankari v. Union of tnd-ia,
(1984) 3 SCC 706l wherein the Court considered three
aspects, one of which is relevant for the present case.
We reproduce the sarne: (SCC pp. 7O7-7O8, para ll
"I- ... We may make it clear that the
judgrnent will not apply to the States of
Andhra Pradesh and Jammu and Kashmir
because at the time of hearing of tl-e main
writ petitions, it was pointed out to us by
the learned advocates appearing on behalf
of those States that there were special
constitutional provisions in regard to them
which would need independent
consideration by this Court."
The aloresaid clarifrcatory order has its owrr
signifrcance, for it undeniably excludes the
applicability of the domicile test stated rt Prad.eep
Jain fPradeep Jainv. Union .of Indio, (f984) 3 SCC
6541 in respect of the State of Andhra Pradesh.
2O. At this stage, it urould be appropriate to refer
to C. Surekhn [C. Surekha v. Union of India, {I9a8l a
SCC 5261. The said case arose from Osmania
University in Andhra Pradesh. The petitioner therein
had passed from tl.e said University and he intended
to take the All India Entrance Examination for
admission to PG medical course in 1988. He had
challenged the constitutional validity of Articles 371-
D(2XbXirr) and (4(4 of the Constitution as well as the
Presidentia-l Order as a consequence of which the
students of Andhra Pradesh have been excluded for
compe ting in the afolqsg{d examination. The ftr''o-

50
Judge Berich referred to the decisions in pradeep
Jain [Pradeep Jainv. [Jnion of Ind.ia, (1984) 3 SCC
654f and Reita Nirankon [Reita Nirankari v. union of
India- (L9a4l 3 SCC 706l and nored the stand of the
Union of India and Andhra Pradesh in their respective
countcr-aflidavits that had asserted that institutions
in the State ofAndhra Pradesh were kept out from the
purview o[ the scheme in view of the decision rendered
in Pradeep Jain [Pradeep Jain v. [Jnion of Ind.ia, egaal
3 SCC 6541 . The Court a.lso took note of the fact that
the issue was kept opcn in Reita Nirankari [Reita
Nirankoi v. Union of India, (t994l 3 SCC 7O6l , a-nd
referred to thc pronouncements in p.
Sambamurthg v. State of A.p. p. Sambamurthg v. State
of A.P., (1987) I SCC 362 : (t9a7l 2 ATC SO2l
, Minerua Mills Ltd. v. [Jnion of India [Minerug_ Mllts
Ltd. v. Union ol Intia, (1980) 3 SCC 625l , S.p.
Som1nth Kumar v. (Jnion of India {5.p. Sampath
Kumorv. Union of India, (1985) 4 SCC 458: l9g5 SCC
(L&S) 9861 and reiteratcd the principle that Article
37 l-D(3) was valid because clause (lO) of Article 37I-D provides as follows: (C. Surekhn case lC.
SurekJto v. Union of India, (1988) 4 SCC 526l , SCC p.
531, para 4)
"4_ ...'37L-D. (31 The provisions of this
Article and o[ any order made by t]re
President thereunder shall have effect
no(wlthstanding anything in any other
provision of this Constitution or in any
other law for the time being in force..'

5t
55. Thereafter, in paragraphs 36 and 37, it was held as
under:
36. We have referred to the aforesaid judgments in
extenso as the learned counsel appearing for the
petitioners have laid immense emphasis that there
cannot be reservation o[ any kind in respect of
postgraduate or super specidlity courses regard being
had to the law laid down by many a judgment of this
Court. It is urged that the State of Andhra Pradesh
and Tel,angana cannot apply the domicile test only to
admit its own students and that too also in respect of
l5% quota meant for non-local candidates. We have
already analysed the factual score and the legal
position. The undivided State of Andhra Pradesh
enjoys a special privilege granted to it under Article
371-D of thc Constitution and the Presidential Order.
The judgments of the larger Bench do not refer to the
said Article nor do they refer to the Presidentia-l Order,
for the said issue did not arise in the said cases.
A scheme has been laid down tn Pradeep
Jain [Pradeep Jain v. Union of Innia, ( 1984] 3 SCC
65a] and the concept of percentage had undergone
certain changes. In Reita Nironkori lReito
Nirankari v. Union ol Indio, (1984) 3 SCC 7O6l , the
samc three-Judge Bench clarified the position which
we have already reproduced hereinbefore. However,
in C. Surekha lC. Surekhav. Union of India, ll9a8l 4
SCC 5261 , the Court had expressed its view about the
amendment o[ the Presidential Order regard being had
to the passage of time and the advancement in the
State of Andhra Pradesh. It has been vehcmcntly

52
urged by Mr Marlapalle ttrat despitc 27 years having
been elapsed, the situation remains the same. We
take note of the said submission and we are also
inclined to ccho the observation t].at was made
in Fo.z.al GLnfoor [Faztl Ghafoor v. union of India,
1988 Supp SCC 794 : I SCEC 356l wherein it has
been stated thus: (SCC p.795, para 2l
"2. ... ln Pradeep Jain co.se [pradeep
Jainv. Union of India, (1984) 3 SCC 6541
this Court has observed that in
superspecialities there should really be no
reservation. This is so in the general
interest of the country and for improving
the standa-rd of higher education and
thereby improving the quality of available
medical services to the peqple-q[-[qdia. W_e
hope and tmst that the Government of
India and thc State Governments shall
seriously considcr this aspect of the matter
without delay and appropriate guidetines
shall be evoh,ed by thc Indian Medical
Council so as to keep thc superspecialities
in medical education unreserved, open arld
free. "
37. The fond hope has remained in the sphere of
hope though there has been a progressive change. The
said privilegc rcmains unchanged, as if to compele
with eternity. Therefore, wc echo the sarne feeting and
reiterate the aspirations of others so that authorities
caJI objectively assess and approach the situation so

53
that the naLional interest can becorne paramount. We
do not intend to add anything in this regard.
56. Thus, on perusal of the order passed by Honble
Supreme Court in Reita Nirankari (supra) and relevant
paragraphs of paragraphs 19, 2O, 36 and 37 of the decision
of Hon'ble Supreme Court in Sandeep (supra), it is evident
that the ratio laid down by the Hon'ble Supreme Court in
Pradeep Jain (supra) does not apply to the State of
Telangana.
57. Now we may advert to the decision of the Hon'ble
Supreme Court in Chebrolu Leela Prasad Rao (supra). The
Hon'ble Supreme Court dealt with the validity of G.O.Ms.
No.3, dated 10.O1.2OO0 issued by erstwtrile State of
Andhra Pradesh which prescribed 1OO% reservation to the
Scheduled Tribe candidates, out of whom 33.1 / 3o/o shall be
wornen for the post of teachers in the school in the
scheduled areas in the State of Andhra Pradesh. The
questions which were answered by Hon'ble Supreme Court
were quoted 1n paragraph 2 of the judgment, which is
reproduced belou,:

)4
2. Several questions have been referred for
consideration in the order dated ll_l_2016 [Clebrolu
Leela Pro-sad Raov. State of A.p.,l2o2tl t I SCC 526l .
We have renumbered euesrions l(a], (bl, (4 and (d)
based on interconncction_ The qucstions are as
follows : (Chebrolu l.eela prasad_ Rao case lChebrotu
Leela Prasad Rao r,. Srate of A.p., (2o2tl 1f SCC 526l ,
SCC 1;. 527, para 1)
(/) What is the scope of para 5(1), Schedule,V to
the Constitution of tndia?
(a) Does the provision empower the Governor to
make a new law?
(bl Does the power extend to subordinate
legislation?
(c) Can the exercise of the power conferred therein
override fundamental rights guaranteed under part
III?
(d) Does thc excrcise of such power override any
parallcl exercisc o[ power by the president under
Article 37 I-D?
(21 Whether lOOTo reservation is permissible under
thc Constitution?
(3) Whether the notification merely contemplates a
classification under Article 16(l) artd not reservation
under Article l6(4)?
(4) Whcthcr the conditions of eligibility (i.e. origin
and cut-ofT datcJ to avail the benefit of reservation in
thc notihcation are reasonable?
58. Relialce has been placed by learned counsel for the
petltroners on ans\,ver given by Hon,ble Supreme Court to

))
question No.2 in paragraphs 145 and 146, which read as
under:
145. A reservation that is permissible by protective
mode, by making it lOO"/o would become
discriminatory and impermissible. The opportunity of
public employment cannot be denied unjustly to the
incumbents, and it is not t]le prerogative of few. The
citizens have equal rights, and the total exclusion of
others by creating an opportunity for one class is not
contemplated by the Founding Fathers of the
Constitution of India. Equality of opportunity and
pursuit of choice under Article 5l-A cannot be
deprived of unjustly and arbitrarily. As per the
Presidential Order, the citizens of ttre locality and
outsiders were entitled to I5%o of emplo5rment in t]-e
district cadre in terms of clause ( lO) of Article
370(i)(d) of the Constitution. Thus, the G.O. does not
classify but deals witll reservations. It was contrary to
the report sent to the President by tlre Govemor,
which indicated even the posts whioh were reserved
for Scheduled Tribes Teachers, they were not available
as such Tribes Advisory Council decided to fill them
from other non-local triba]s.
146. We find that GOMs No. 3 of 2OOO is wholly
impermissible and cannot be said to be legally
permissiblc and constitutionally valid. It can be said
that action is not only irrational, but it violates the
rights guaranteed under Part III of the Constitution
and is not sustainable.

56
59. Nou, we may advert to the decision of the Hontrle
Supreme Court in Satyajit Kumar (supra). In the aforesaid
decision, the Honble Supreme Court dealt with the validity
of advertisement dated 28.12.2016 issued by Department
of Personnel and Administrative Reforms and Raj Basha of
Government of Jharkhand, inviting applications for
appointment to the post of trained graduate teachers in
government seconda5z schools to the extent of looo/o
reservation for the local candidates/ residents of thirteen
scheduled areas in the State of Jharkhand. The Hon,ble
Supreme Court in paragraphs 99 and IOO held as under:
99. Even under Articlc 16(3) of the Constitution of
India, it is the Parliament alone, which is authorized
to make any law prescribing, in regard to a class or
classes of employment or appointment to arl omce
under thc Government of, or any local or other
authority within, a State of Union Territory, any
requircment as to re sidcnce within the State or Union
territory prior to such employment or appointment. As
per Article 35 o[ thc Constitution of India,
notwithstanding anything contained in ttre
Constitution, the parliament shalt have and the
kgistature of a State sha]I not have the power to
make laws with respect to any of the matters which,
under clause (3) of Article l6 may be provided for lanv
made by Parhament. There[ore, irnpugncd

57
Notification/ Order making 1OO7" reservation.for the
local resident of the concemed Scheduled
Area/Districts (reservation on the basis of resident) is
ultra vires to Article 35 r/w Article 16{3) of the
Constitution oI India.
1OO. Applying thc law laid down by this Court in
the case of Clrcbrolu l.eela Prasad Rao (supra) and in
view of the above discussion and for the reasons
stated above, the High Court has not committed any
error in concluding and holding that the Notification
No. 5938 and Order No- 5939 d.ated. 14.7.2OL6 issued
by the State Government providing IOO%o reservation
for the local residents o[ concerned Scheduled
Districts/Areas as being unconstitutional and ultra
vircs Articles L4, L3(2|., 15 and 16(2) of the
Constitution o[ India. It is rightly observed and held
that said Notification and Order would also violate
Articles t6(31 and 35(a-i) of the Constitution of India.
Thc High Court has also rightly observed and held
that aforesaid Notification and Order is ultra wires to
paragraph 5(11 of the Filth Schedule of the
Constitution of lndia. We are in complete agreernent
with the view taken by the High Court.
60. The decision of Hon'ble Supreme Court in Pradeep
Jain (supra) does not apply to the State of Telangana, as
clarified by Hon'ble Supreme Court itself in Reita
Nirankari (supra) and Sandeep (supra). The decisions of
Honble Supreme Court in Chebrolu Leela Prasad Rao

58
(supra) and Satyajit Kumar (supra) are authorities for the
proposition that 1OO% reservation in matter oI employment
is violative of the constitutional guarantee contained in
Article 16 of the Constitution of India. However, we may
reiterate that instant case is not a case of 10O% reservation
as only 85% of competent authority quota seats in respect
of educational institutions which have been set up after
formation of the State, i.e. , 02 .06 .2O I 4 had been reserved
for the caldidates of State of Telangana. Therefore, the
decisions of Hon'bte Supreme Court in Chebrolu Leela
Prasad Rao (supra) and Satyajit Kumar (supra) have no
application to the lacts of these cases.
61. Therefore, the issue No,(vi) is answered by stating
that reservation to the extent of lOOoh in favour of local
candidates in the Sate of Telangana by way of amendment
to tlre 2OL7 Rules had not been provided.
62. However, in vieu, of submission made by learned
Advocate General and learned standing counsel for the
University that 85% of competent authority quota seats
alone had been reserved for local candiclates for the State

I
of Telangana in respect of institutions sct up aftcr
formation of the State i.e., O2.06.2.01 4 and ir is permissible
for the students of other States ir-rcluding the State of
Andhra Pradesh to participatc in l Sol, ot competent
authority quota seats, it is direcrcd that the aforesaid
provision, if not already madc, shall be madc in the seat
matrix notified by the University.
63. In vieu, of preceding analysis, u,e do r_rot find any
merit in these writ petitions. In the result, the same fail
and are accordingly dismissed.
